Surprise!

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

If you want to keep people on their toes, make them think you're constantly watching.

Persistence, after all, usually pays off. Consider, for example, the lawyers you know who may not be all that bright but do well anyway because people will do anything to make them go away.

This occurred to me after a federal judge ruled that the Trump administration canât bar members of Congress from making unannounced visits to immigration detention facilities (aka concentration camps, depending on your point of view).

The Department of Homeland Security had been requiring seven daysâ notice for congressional visits.

What do you think they were doing during that seven-day period? Vacuuming? Cleaning up the dog hair?

Or maybe, if youâre being cynical, theyâre hiding the real conditions in those prisons.

I wonât presume, but it seems to me that concerned Congresspeople arenât going about this the right way. They might win in court â at least until the endless appeals kick in â but the occasional surprise visit isnât going to make a lot of difference.

Congress needs to visit every day. Every single day. Twice a day if possible.

Itâs not that hard. I know it seems hard, but itâs not really if you go about it properly.

Prior to last weekâs election there were 214 Democrats in the House. Now there are a lot more. There are approximately the same amount of ICE detention centers. You only need one Congressperson per facility.

Forget litigating the weekâs notice requirement. Just provide those notices for every single day for every detention center after the first week. Theyâll have to clean up every single day.

Yes, thereâs a lot of ground to cover but, remember, these inspections should be surprising. Itâs just as much of a surprise if you donât show up. Consider how annoying it is for your in-laws to cancel after youâve spent the week tidying up.

Keep them on their toes. Deploy those hundreds of overseers in a random, unexpected way. Cover as many detention centers as possible â some on consecutive days, others when least expected. Use every bit of your personpower (that should be a word, shouldnât it?).

Get serious, organize, and be as annoying as possible.

OK, I realize this wonât happen, but the point is that litigation isnât always the right answer. You just have to be loud, annoying and incredibly persistent.

The election. Maybe Citizens United v. Federal Election Commission wasnât such a bad thing after all.

As most of you probably know, that was the 2010 Supreme Court decision that money was a form of speech that could be endlessly spent on elections. A lot of people have been moaning about the corrupting influence of money on politics ever since.

But look at what happened last week: Candidates backed by enormous amounts of money got beat. Many of them got beat badly, at least in part because voters were mad about the insanely rich people backing them.

So we should be encouraging billionaires and a trillionaire to keep on trying to buy elections. Itâs self-defeating.

And if nothing else, it makes them slightly less rich.

As for the visitors, only just dragging themselves over the finishing line last season as they clung to their League Two status, Tranmere were simply hoping for smoother sailing this time around. Still recovering from a long-term knee injury, Tranmere will once again be without the services of veteran striker Connor Jennings on Tuesday night. Likewise, former Bournemouth youngster Zane Okoro remains on the sidelines with a muscle issue and won’t play any part here.

With their sights set on securing an iconic Championship promotion this season, Stockport certainly find the mood in their camp at a red-hot high. Winning each of their last four consecutive showdowns at Edgeley Park by an aggregate score of 7-2, the Hatters are rightfully installed as a standout contender on Tuesday night. Landing a 3-1 win when they last met Tranmere for an FA Cup showdown 12 months ago, we are expecting a similar outcome here.

Casualties: Unknown introduces you to a harsh world where you’ll need to adapt and descend. While going down, injuries are unavoidable. The game follows a simulated limb and cardiovascular system, and you’ll have to bandage yourself, fight infections, and stabilize the blood loss.

Morgan Wallen, Garth Brooks, Taylor Swift & More Artists With Most Weeks at No. 1 on Top Country Albums Chart: Full List

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Male solo artists dominate, though three female solo artists and two groups have also achieved the feat.

Morgan Wallen’s I’m the Problem leads Billboard‘s Top Country Albums chart (dated Nov. 14) for the 52nd nonconsecutive week. This ups Wallen’s career tally of weeks at No. 1 to 238, allowing him to extend his lead as the artist with the most weeks at No. 1 in the chart’s 62-year history.

Wallen’s achievement is especially impressive because he has amassed this hefty total of weeks at No. 1 in such a short amount of time. He first topped the chart a little more than six years ago — on Aug. 15, 2020.

I’m the Problem is Wallen’s third album to log a full year at No. 1 on Top Country Albums. Dangerous: The Double Album clung to the top spot for 97 weeks from 2021-23. One Thing at a Time led for 87 weeks from 2023-25.

Top Country Albums originated as Hot Country Albums in the Jan. 11, 1964 issue of Billboard. Johnny Cash’s Ring of Fire (The Best of Johnny Cash) headed the inaugural chart. The compilation was well-timed: “Ring of Fire” had headed Hot Country Songs for seven weeks the previous summer and had crossed over to reach the top 20 on the Billboard Hot 100.

The chart was named Hot Country LP’s from 1968 to 1984, when vinyl LPs were king.

Twenty-one artists have logged 50 or more weeks at No. 1 on Top Country Albums. Male solo artists dominate, as you might expect, though three female solo artists have achieved the feat, as have two groups. (One of those groups is an all-female group.) Only one Black artist has amassed 50 or more weeks at No. 1, though that may change as country becomes more inclusive.

Here’s a look at all artists who have topped Top Country Albums for 50 or more weeks.

Longest-running No. 1: When the Sun Goes Down (14 weeks in 2004)

Notes: Chesney’s tally of No. 1 albums includes two live albums, LIVE: Live Those Songs Again (2006) and Live in No Shoes Nation (2017).

Longest-running No. 1: Blue (28 weeks in 1996-97)

Notes: Rimes’ total includes a 1997 compilation of early recordings, Unchained Melody: The Early Years. Rimes was just 14 in February 1997 when she won a Grammy for best new artist. She remains the youngest winner ever in that category.

Longest-running No. 1: My World (17 weeks in 1965-66)

Notes: My World was the first country album to receive a Grammy nod for album of the year. The album included the crossover hit “Make the World Go Away,” which all but defined the “countrypolitan” sound.

Longest-running No. 1: Wichita Lineman (20 weeks in 1968-69)

Notes: Wichita Lineman is tied with Johnny Cash at San Quentin for the longest run at No. 1 of any album in the 1960s. Campbell’s tally of No. 1 albums also includes Bobbie Gentry & Glen Campbell, a collab with the singer of “Ode to Billie Joe.” The 1968 album was the first collaborative album to reach No. 1 on the chart.

Longest-running No. 1: Pancho & Lefty (with Willie Nelson) (eight weeks in 1983)

Notes: Haggard’s tally of No. 1s includes eight albums on which his backing group, The Strangers, was also credited. Hag’s tally includes three live albums, Okie From Muskogee (1970), The Fightin’ Side of Me (1970)and I Love Dixie Blues … So I Recorded ‘Live’ in New Orleans (1973). It also includes two collabs with Willie Nelson, Pancho & Lefty and Django and Jimmie (2015).

Most recent week at No. 1: April 12, 2014

Longest-running No. 1: Johnny Cash at San Quentin (20 weeks in 1969)

Notes: Cash’s No. 1 albums spanned more than 50 years. Johnny Cash at San Quentin (which received a Grammy nod for album of the year) has had the longest run at No. 1 of any live album. It is also tied with Glen Campbell’s Wichita Lineman as the longest-running No. 1 album of the 1960s. Cash also reached No. 1 with two other live albums – Johnny Cash at Folsom Prison (1968) and The Johnny Cash Show (1970), which was recorded at the Grand Ole Opry. He also topped the chart with a 1985 collab LP with Willie Nelson, Waylon Jennings and Kris Kristofferson and with two posthumous albums – American V: A Hundred Highways (2006) and Out Among the Stars (2014).

Most recent week at No. 1: April 20, 1968

Longest-running No. 1: I’ve Got a Tiger by the Tail (15 weeks in 1965)

Notes: Owens’ first No. 1 album, Buck Owens Sings Tommy Collins (1964), was a tribute album to the singer/songwriter whose biggest hits included “You Better Not Do That” and “If You Can’t Bite, Don’t Growl.” Owens’ tally of No. 1 albums includes two live albums, Carnegie Hall Concert (1966) and Buck Owens and His Buckaroos in Japan! (1967).

Most recent week at No. 1: April 13, 2019

Longest-running No. 1s: Ocean Front Property (six weeks in 1987) and Carrying Your Love With Me (six weeks in 1997)

Notes: Strait made this list the hard way – without a single album that logged more than six weeks at No. 1. Strait’s tally of No. 1 albums includes the 1992 soundtrack to Pure Country, in which he starred.

Longest-running No. 1: Fly (36 weeks in 1999-2000)

Notes: The Chicks, formerly Dixie Chicks, are the second-highest ranking group on this list. Three of the group’s No. 1 albums – Fly, Home and Taking the Long Way – received Grammy nods for album of the year. The latter album won the award. The group’s most recent No. 1 album, Gaslighter, was bumped from the top spot by the debut album by a promising newcomer named Morgan Wallen.

Longest-running No. 1: Greatest Hits (16 weeks in 1979)

Notes: Jennings’ tally of No. 1 albums includes three collabs with Nelson – one just with Nelson; one that also included Jessi Colter and Tompall Glaser; and one that included Johnny Cash and Kris Kristofferson as well. His tally also includes a 1976 live album, Waylon Live.

Longest-running No. 1: Not a Moment Too Soon (29 weeks in 1994)

Notes: McGraw’s tally includes a 2017 collab with his wife, Faith Hill, The Rest of Our Life. That title should probably be The Rest of Our Lives, but we’re not going to correct the grammar of someone who has logged 76 weeks at No. 1.

Longest-running No. 1: Always & Forever (43 weeks in 1987-88)

Notes: Always & Forever had the longest run at No. 1 of any album in the 1980s.

Longest-running No. 1s: Charley Pride Sings Heart Songs (16 weeks in 1972) and The Best of Charley Pride, Volume 2 (16 weeks in 1972).

Notes:  The albums referenced above were No. 1 back-to-back and spent a combined total of 32 consecutive weeks at No. 1. Now, that’s what you call dominating a chart. Both albums contained Pride’s signature song, “Kiss an Angel Good Mornin.’” Pride’s final No. 1 album, There’s a Little Bit of Hank in Me (1980), was a tribute to Hank Williams. Pride is the only Black artist on this list so far.

Longest-running No. 1: This One’s for You (50 weeks in 2017-18)

Notes: This One’s for You had the longest run at No. 1 of any album in the 2010s.

Longest-running No. 1: Come on Over (50 weeks in 1997-2000)

Notes: Twain is the second-highest ranking female solo artist on this list. Come on Over had the longest run at No. 1 of any album in the 1990s. The hit-studded blockbuster spent time at No. 1 in four consecutive years – 1997 through 2000. It received a Grammy nod for album of the year, and spawned two song of the year nominees, “You’re Still the One” and “You’ve Got a Way.”

Longest-running No. 1: Kenny (25 weeks in 1979-80)

Notes: Kenny, which housed the hit “You Decorated My Life,” had the longest run at No. 1 of any album in the 1970s. Rogers’ tally includes a 1978 collab with Dottie West, Every Time Two Fools Collide, and a 2018 compilation, The Best of Kenny Rogers: Through the Years, which hit No. 1 following his death in March 2020.

Most recent week at No. 1: July 29, 2023?

Longest-running No. 1: Fearless (35 weeks in 2008-09)

Notes:  Swift is the highest-charting female solo artist on this list, which is especially impressive given her mid-career pivot to pop. Her tally includes both the original and Taylor’s Version’s editions of Fearless, Speak Now and Red. With Fearless, Swift became the youngest Grammy winner for album of the year to that point, a title now held by Billie Eilish.

Longest-running No. 1: Always on My Mind (22 weeks in 1982)

Notes: Nelson’s tally includes a collab with Waylon Jennings, Jessi Colter and Tompall Glaser; another with Jennings, Johnny Cash and Kris Kristofferson; another with just Jennings, and two with Haggard. It also includes two live albums, Willie and Family Live (1979) and the Honeysuckle Rose soundtrack (1980).

Longest-running No. 1s: Feels So Right (28 weeks in 1981-82) and Mountain Music (28 weeks in 1982-83).

Notes: Alabama is the highest-ranking group on this list. Feels So Right (in May 1981) was the first No. 1 by a group that wasn’t named after its leader. (So, we’re discounting Merle Haggard and the Strangers, Sonny James and the Southern Gentlemen and The Charlie Daniels Band.) Alabama’s tally of No. 1 albums includes a 1988 live album, Alabama Live.

Longest-running No. 1: No Fences (41 weeks in 1990-91)

Notes: Brooks’ tally of No. 1 albums includes a double-disc live album, Double Live (1998); a pair of Christmas albums, Garth Brooks & the Magic of Christmas (1999) and Christmas Together (2016), the latter a collab with his wife, Trisha Yearwood; and a remarkable four box sets – The Limited Series (1998), Blame It All on My Roots: Five Decades of Influences (2013), The Ultimate Collection (2016) and The Anthology Part I: The First Five Years (2017).

Longest-running No. 1: Dangerous: The Double Album (97 weeks in 2021-23)

Notes: Dangerous: The Double Album had the longest run at No. 1 in the chart’s history. One Thing at a Time, which spent 87 weeks at No. 1, is in second place.

On Ibuprofen Over Mather Pass — John Muir Trail Day 11

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

I awoke in the dark and went to get my bear cannister containing my breakfast.

Every day on the trail I have granola with dried milk and freeze-dried fruit.  The best version includes both strawberries and blueberries.  I am surprised at how quickly the fruit absorbs the water; unlike dinner, there is no waiting for breakfast.  I am also amazed at how tart and juicy the fruit becomes.  Even having the same thing day after day, I don’t grow tired of it.

I suddenly recalled seeing Sally, the hiker who set up near me after nightfall, earlier on the trail the day before.  She was descending slowly and gingerly, as if she were injured.  This morning she was already up and, when I asked her, she acknowledged having trouble with her ankle and being worried she might not be able to finish the John Muir Trail.

We chatted for quite a while.  She told me she was of Chinese origin, which I had guessed from her accent, but she now lived in Brisbane, Australia.  When she had her second child in China, she felt it was necessary to leave, because that was during the time of China’s one-child policy.  I did not ask her, but am pretty confident that “Sally” was not her birth name.

She brought her two children to Australia.  One was now a doctor, like her husband, and the other was an ophthalmologist.  Her husband supported her going on adventures and she had hiked all over the world.  Most amazing, she summited Aconcagua, which at an elevation of “only” 22,837 feet is the highest point in the Western Hemisphere!  “Quite a remarkable person,” I thought.

A situation like that is interesting.  We were both going north on the John Muir Trail headed for the same destination in Happy Valley, Yosemite.  With Serena and Evelyn, I was going north and they were going south.  It was a safe bet I would never see them again.  But with Sally, it is much harder to tell.  I might never see her again, or I might see her repeatedly, all the way to the finish line.  Time would tell.

I made a cathole on the hill above my tent the night before; then Sally arrived, so I was a little embarrassed how that might work in the morning.  But she was faster than I was and broke camp well before I did.  About the time she left, the sun was starting to creep down the mountainside on the other side of the lake.

Still in shadow on our side of Lake Marjorie; bright sunshine on the opposite shore.

In any case, I was able to go and do my business without worry and then head out.  Once again, I was late — it was already eight a.m. — even though I was up when it was still quite dark.

I am just a slow packer, what can I say?   But I expected a slightly easier day and 8:00 a,m. was bad, but not terrible.  I knew I would be starting downhill in similar fashion to the previous day but would only be dropping down to 10,000 feet before climbing again to Mather Pass.  With the prospect of going down, I took another Ibuprofen for my injured knee.

My knee did twinge from time to time, but it was functioning, so I kept descending and eventually reached the bottom.  A big river was located there, and I had read scary reports of people wading across with difficulty.  This late in the season, however, the levels were low enough that even in the big rivers, there were almost always places that you could cross by steppingstones.  With hiking poles for balance, those were usually not difficult.

Someone had placed a straight and deliberate line of rocks where the trail crosses the stream.

Here the rocks are more random and require a moment’s thought to discern the best route across.

There ensued a pretty level portion of trail that I enjoyed, having only a slight upward slope following the river.   At least ascending, my knee liked it better.  Apparently, my knee likes going uphill but doesn’t like going downhill.  I wish my lungs felt the same way, but when it gets steep, they start to wheeze and gasp.

Quite level, wih few trail obstacles — a trail like this invites a faster pace.

I kept chugging along, keeping one foot in front of the other as gradually the miles went by.  The trail eventually emerged from the forest, into a thinner type of forest, and finally into open landscapes.  The views of the mountains enclosing this valley filled me with awe.

In this valley, and all along the John Muir Trail, there were views like this in all directions.

Finally, I caught up to Sally.  She left camp almost an hour ahead of me, so I was making a faster pace.

I also saw Amy again, drying out her socks following a fall-in-the-creek mishap she suffered the day before.  It seemed that Amy and Sally were people I might continue to see from time to time.

It would not make sense for us to hike together, however, because they both have a very steady hiking speed, which is slower than mine.  Our similar net speed comes from my hiking fast, but then stopping frequently to admire the landscape, or take a photograph, or catch my breath.  It would frustrate both sides to try hiking together.

There was a little breeze, so despite the sun beating down, the zephyr kept it a slightly cooler.  As I got to the end of a sort of lunar landscape of boulders, it was not clear where on the ridge above me lay Mather Pass.  It turned out it was above a huge rockslide, but the trail was cut into the rock of the slide.  It ascended through many switchbacks.  At exactly one o’clock, five hours after starting, I stood on the top of Mather Pass.

While I ate lunch in the pass, Sally caught up and joined me for a time.  Then I started going down the other side, which everyone had said was rockier.  It was rockier.

I was really worried about my injured knee.  So, I descended carefully until suddenly, the trail just disappeared.   I was confused for a little while until I looked beyond a jumble of boulders and realized that the path continued on the other side of the rock fall.  The slide had completely obscured the trail.  I couldn’t say how long ago it happened, but trail workers maintain these popular trails, so I expect it was within the past year.  You would not have wanted to be on trail when these enormous boulders came rolling down the mountain!

How did I get through?  As everyone else did, I suppose, I devised my own route over the cascade of rocks and eventually found a way through to resume hiking the trail.  One key path-finding method on the John Muir Trail is to follow the trail of horse manure from the pack trains.

The Appalacian Trail has white blazes. The John Muir Trail has… this.

I really wondered how those animals managed to pick their way through the boulders with hoofs instead of hands to grip with!  On my way lower, I warned people going southbound toward Mather Pass of the obstacle that lay ahead for them.

At this point I thought, “Well, I made the high point of the pass at one o’clock and I’m only about two miles from my projected tent site.  Why don’t I try to log a little more distance today?  Then I won’t have as much pressure tomorrow and the day after.”  So, I continued on past the first Palisade Lake where I had planned to camp.

As I hiked, the sun grew more powerful until I finally put my umbrella up again.  I only used it for about two hours, but it definitely cooled me off a bit, and preserved some of my energy.

Amy and Sally and I had separately decided to press on further, but I came across Amy making camp quite soon after first Palisade Lake.  She said her legs just didn’t have it that day, so she had taken one of the tent spots by the trail.

Meanwhile, I continued on, now past the second Palisade Lake.  It was getting later in the afternoon, but a southbound hiker told me that down on the Staircase there were a number of individual tent sites.  At first I thought, “Well, maybe I’ll make it to the Staircase.”  This was a steep downhill stretch that, despite that hiker’s assurances, seemed unlikely to have much room for tent sites.

It was after four p.m., so then I reconsidered, “If I find a suitable site before the Staircase, I will play it safe and stay there.”

I looked at one spot that didn’t feel quite right, but then I saw another one on the other side of the trail.  It was nice and sandy and flat.

Seeing it in the bright sunshine, I assessed that it would work really well and I set up my tent.  Then I went and found a place to make a cathole, to prepare for the next morning.  Returning, I saw a couple of guys passing my tent site and realized it was not quite as private as I had believed.  But by then I was set up, and it was late afternoon, and I figured there would not be many more passersby that evening.

I made a lasagna for dinner.  Oh my God.  Was it a delicious lasagna?  Yes!  No, I’m joking, it was pretty awful.  However, I had learned enough to make it in the packet rather than create a gooey mess in my Nalgene container.  Cleaning that container had been a nightmare the last time I tried Italian food in it.

So, I ate the lasagna out of the packet, which worked okay, except that my spoon was not long enough to reach the bottom of the packet.  It made for messy eating.  I decided that for non-Italian dishes I would go back to my trusty Nalgene.

Back in the tent, I sent out my daily message on the Garmin, assuring people that I was okay.  In getting ready to turn in, I discovered an enormous blister on the second toe of my right foot.

I don’t know why, but the blister looked like it was going to explode nuclearly at any moment.  It was not really painful, but I looked in my first aid kit to see if there were any instructions.  Do you pop such a blister?  Do you leave it alone?  How do you treat it?  The directions were ambiguous, so I decided to leave it alone.

As for my knee, it seemed to have responded well to the Ibuprofen so far.  Just before sleep, I would take another ibuprofen, and another in the morning before the Staircase.  But then I might need to start cadging Ibuprofen from fellow hikers, because I would be running out.

The knee injury was two days old and it still felt tender.  My sense was that I faced serious risk if I incurred further injury; if there were none, however, I would be fine.

Camped on my rocky knoll, surrounded by mountains, it was a beautiful campsite.  Those mountains meant the sun departed early, but the sky remained blue for a long time after that.  I enjoyed the peaceful spot, and my slightly shorter day, and with my phone plugged into the charger block, I fell asleep.

Hi, I’m Papa George! Prediction: Oxford United vs Cheltenham Town

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Two camps who will turn their attention back to EFL Trophy action on Tuesday night, Oxford United will welcome Cheltenham to the Kassam Stadium.

Although Europe’s elite domestic leagues might be in the midst of November’s international break, there are still plenty of eye-catching betting promotions available.

Ahead of Tuesday’s EFL Trophy showdown, you can find all of the key Oxford United vs Cheltenham Town H2H stats here.

Verdict: Oxford United win & Under 4.5 goals

Starting with the hosts, losing their Championship status last season, it is no secret that Oxford’s sole focus is on booking an immediate second-tier return this time around. While Aaron Ramsey’s side might have watched their FA Cup adventure come to a frustrating end over the weekend as they eventually slumped to a 1-0 loss against Stevenage, the Yellows are firmly scrapping it out at the top of the League One standings. Yet to feature since the start of October and recently undergoing surgery on a long-term hamstring injury, Cheltenham have already confirmed that summer recruit and former MK Dons midfielder Joe Tomlinson will not feature for the remainder of 2026.

Making a speedy return from a muscle injury that his sustained against Tottenham’s U21s last month, veteran goalkeeper Joe Day could return between the sticks. Bagging an EFL Trophy brace against Spurs’ academy on October 13th, George Miller should be handed a full recall on Tuesday night.

While Oxford might have watched their extended purple patch come to an end over the weekend, Tuesday’s hosts will certainly be smelling the chance to claim an immediate response here. With their sights fixed on booking an immediate Championship return this season, Ramsey’s camp find themselves pencilled in as a standout contender in midweek. Losing just one of their previous 12 straight appearances across all competitions within 90 minutes, Oxford will be looking to exploit a Cheltenham squad who have shown plenty of cause for concern on their travels.

Verdict: Oxford United win & Under 4.5 goals

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang admits to having extramarital affair with suspected Chinese spy

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Nvidia Corp. said on Sunday it’s launching an “independent investigation” into reports that its founder and Chief Executive Jensen Huang had been targeted by a foreign intelligence plot, shortly after he admitted he “had been intimate” with a Singapore-based executive whom U.S. officials accuse of spying for China.

In response to the allegations, the Republican-led foreign affairs and intelligence committee of the U.S. Congress has asked Huang (pictured, right) to testify this week at a closed-door briefing on the chipmaker’s “security protocols against foreign adversary espionage.” The request came after the New York Times reported last month that the CEO had been targeted in a Chinese espionage operation.

The accused spy is a woman called Alice Huang or Huang Le (left), of no relation to the Nvidia CEO. Alice Huang is the CEO of a Singapore-based data center company called Megaspeed. It had previously been suspected of smuggling Nvidia’s high-end graphics processing units to China, prompting U.S. officials to begin monitoring her. Those officials have apparently determined that Alice Huang is an asset of the Chinese intelligence services, tasked with passing commercial secrets back to Beijing.

In a statement earlier today, Nvidia’s board of directors said that though it has no reason to believe that either U.S. national security or the company’s secrets were compromised, its inquiry will examine all available information. “The Board prioritizes U.S. national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of Nvidia and its shareholders,” the statement said. “The Board has confidence in Jensen and his leadership team and will provide further updates as appropriate.”

The company added that Huang supports the investigation into what happened and has vowed to cooperate fully.

A report by Axios earlier today cited a draft email written by Huang to company employees, in which he admitted to having an illicit romantic relationship with Alice Huang and took full responsibility for his actions. “I was unfaithful to my wife,” Huang wrote. “My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did.”

U.S. intelligence chiefs will be concerned because Nvidia’s powerful chips sit at the center of the artificial intelligence boom and give America a critical advantage over its geopolitical rivals. The company’s dominance of AI computing means that Huang is one of the most influential people in the industry, and he has forged close ties with the Trump administration.

The alleged spy reportedly tried to take advantage of Huang’s close links to America’s policymakers. Not only was she tasked with learning more about the company’s secrets, but also persuading Huang to lobby for the company to be allowed to ship its most advanced processors to China.

It’s not clear how much influence she was able to exert on Huang, but last year he successfully petitioned the Trump administration to allow more GPUs to be exported to China. He later struck a deal to sell limited numbers of the company’s second-most powerful chips to China.

So far, nothing has been said of Huang potentially losing his job or being removed from the company, but Yorkville Ives analyst Dan Ives told Gizmodo that such an outcome is possible. “This will add risk to the stock as he is so important to the Nvidia story and strategy globally,” the analyst said.

The investigation follows a rough week for technology firms on Wall Street, which have weathered a selloff that began earlier this month and gained traction after a series of disappointing earnings results in recent days. So far this month, the tech-heavy Nasdaq index has lost almost $1 trillion in value.

Nvidia’s stock has fallen 21% over the last month and is now basically flat in the year to date. Its market capitalization sits at just over $4.5 trillion, down from its peak of just over $5.7 trillion in October.

Amorim wants his Milan players to feel like ‘losing is the end of the world’

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Ruben Amorim has urged his AC Milan players to keep on winning and remind themselves that "losing is the end of the world" after recording a 2-1 win away to Genoa in Serie A.

First-half goals from Alphadjo Cisse and Christian Pulisic were enough for Milan, who move up to fourth in the league standings, five points behind leaders Lazio.

After three seasons in which they did not surpass 22 points after the first 11 matchdays, Milan have now reached 24 points in Serie A this season, recording their best start since 2022-23 (26 points).

Amorim's men have now picked up three wins and three draws in their first six away games in the league this season. It is the fourth time they have remained unbeaten in their first six away matches of a top-flight campaign in the last seven Serie A seasons.

Speaking to DAZN Italia, Amorim said: "I am proud again with my players.

"There are moments when we change the game; we go for the second ball and play different types of football. That is the sign of a great team, who can play different ways during the same match."

However, the Portuguese has offered a cautionary tale to his players after Mikael Ellertsson threatened to ruin the party with a late strike.

"It's about the mentality. When you play for Milan, there is no day off," added the former Manchester United head coach.

"It's not physical; it's about having that panic of losing. That does not mean being afraid of losing; it's feeling that losing is the end of the world. That is the feeling I want in my team, so it depends on that."

8 Nintendo Titles That Should Get The Ocarina of Time Remake Treatment

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Aden Carter is a Contributor at DualShockers who has been covering games professionally since 2020. He specializes in lists and guides, with a focus on RPGs, horror games, survival horror, Soulslikes, cozy/sim games, and action-adventure titles. Before joining DualShockers, Aden contributed to GameRant, XDA, Gamepur, I

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is one of the most beloved games ever created. Fans of this game talk about it with passion, remembering it as a key part of their childhood. The remake shows that fans still love this title, even almost 30 years after the original’s release. It also introduces new features and controls.

Of course, this isn’t the only game that Nintendo has ever made. Nintendo is known for creating many timeless games that fans continue to go back to year after year.

While they may not be as beloved as Ocarina of Time, these games definitely deserve to get the same treatment, breathing new life into them.

Super Mario Sunshine is one of those Nintendo titles that divides fans. There are many who love it, but then there are many who criticize the title for various reasons. Some of the reasons include the messy controls, and the dislike of using FLUDD constantly.

However, there is a large fanbase who misses this game - and I am not just saying that because it is one of my favorite Mario games. I’ve talked to many people who have said that they would love to see a full remake of Super Mario Sunshine.

Plus, remaking the game would allow Nintendo to fix some of the issues that it has. The team could make FLUDD more entertaining to use, fix the messy controls, and update the core gameplay to better fit the newer Mario titles.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time may be the most beloved Zelda title out there, but The Legend of Zelda: Majora’s Mask is a close second, with many fans saying they love it almost as much as Ocarina of Time.

Majora’s Mask is the edgy older brother of Ocarina of Time, giving players a darker story that is full of twists and turns. Plus, it is a direct sequel to Ocarina of Time, so it feels like a no-brainer to continue making remakes by giving Majora’s Mask the treatment given to its little brother.

After seeing Ocarina of Time in this new light, fans would go crazy if they could play the game’s sequel shortly after. There are plenty of other Zelda games worth revisiting, too.

The remake's dev team included younger folks, several of whom never played the original Ocarina of Time, who provided a much-needed modern view of things.

If there are two Pokémon games that I hear mentioned all the time, they are Pokémon Colosseum and Pokémon XD: Gale of Darkness. Gale of Darkness is the one I played a lot as a child, and is also typically the one I hear people talk about more.

Of course, both of these games deserve the Ocarina of Time treatment, but I think that Gale of Darkness deserves it a bit more. This game is one of the best Pokémon spin-offs, with it focusing on an evil organization that is creating shadow Pokémon.

More importantly, they created a Shadow Lugia and want to take over the world. Along with an actual evil organization, the game features a fun battle system that could use some updating to make it more modern. Plus, they could add all of the Pokémon to the game to make things more interesting.

Back in the days of the GameBoy Color, there were two titles that were ahead of their time. I am, of course, referring to The Legend of Zelda: Oracle of Ages and Oracle of Seasons. Both were fantastic titles for the time.

The best part about them is that they were linked together, expanding the story for those who played through both of them. When you beat one, you got a password. When you typed that password into the other game, it linked the two. When you beat the second title, it gives you a password to take back into the first game. This allows you to get the true ending.

It was an interesting mechanic that games didn’t typically do back then. Now, if Nintendo brings these titles back, makes them 3D or HD-2D, then the fan base would go wild and buy both of them. Plus, new technology means you can link the saves without the need of a password.

Eiji Aonuma and the development team for the Ocarina of Time remake discussed how one of the original's infamous dungeons is being improved.

One of the classic platformers from the N64 era is none other than Donkey Kong 64. It is a beloved title that could seriously use updating. After all, the Nintendo 64 wasn’t known for being the smoothest gameplay, and that is prevalent with DK64.

Despite the clunkiness, there is a huge soft spot for this title, and it is one that brings the entire crew together, allowing you to play as DK, Diddy, Lanky, Chunky, and more.

Nintendo brought back the idea of Donkey Kong being in a 3D platformer with Donkey Kong Bananza in June 2025, and it sold decently well, revealing that players still enjoy seeing this ape in his own games. If Nintendo wants to keep bringing back the classics, then this should be a serious consideration.

Earthbound is not a game that I personally grew up with, but it is one that I have always been told to try, and one that I know means a lot to the Nintendo fan base. I’ve met individuals who play it on a yearly basis because of how much they love it. It remains one of the memorable SNES role-playing games.

With all that being said, the game could use some serious updating. Its old-school RPG turn-based combat doesn’t hold up to today’s standard. Imagine if Nintendo gave this game the HD-2D treatment, or even decided to make the game fully 3D.

Either way, it would look fantastic, and it would mean the world to those who grew up with this beloved title and played it since then.

The first of the Fire Emblem games to be released in the west was simply titled Fire Emblem, but it is also known as Fire Emblem: The Blazing Blade, and it follows the story of Lyn, Eliwood, and Hector.

I remember getting this game as a child and falling in love with it. Since then, Fire Emblem has become one of my favorite game series from Nintendo. There are many who say that Path of Radiance is their favorite game, and it holds up well, but if Nintendo is going to go down the remake route, I think it would be best to start with the first game that was brought over to the west. This is just one highlight of many excellent Fire Emblem games.

The Fire Emblem series has seen some great success, with the latest of the games - Fire Emblem: Fortune’s Weave - selling over 1.3 million copies in a month. It is a series that has been beloved for decades, and it deserves to see one of the earlier titles brought back in either HD-2D or full 3D like the modern games.

There is no doubt that one of the most popular games Nintendo has ever made is Super Mario 64, and it has a special place in history. This game set the standards for movement and camera control in a 3D space.

Super Mario 64 is one of the most well-known games that was released on the N64, with it only being second to maybe Ocarina of Time. Not only that, but it inspired multiple games that came after.

If there is any game that Nintendo has created outside of The Legend of Zelda series that deserves a full remake with modernized graphics, it would be Super Mario 64. Almost any fan of Nintendo would say that a remake of this game would be a no-brainer.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is one of the most beloved games of all time, but these remakes might be just as meaningful to fans.

‘Steps’ Review: A Stellar Voice Cast Including Amanda Seyfried, Ali Wong and Bette Midler Gets Stuck in a Tortured Revisionist Fairy Tale

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Stephanie Hsu, Nikki Glaser, Daniel Radcliffe and Peter Dinklage also feature in Netflix’s animated overhaul of the ‘Cinderella’ story, told from the perspective of her “evil” stepsister.

No doubt there are worse ways for families to kill a couple of hours together over Thanksgiving, but pity the adults forced to keep the kids company through Netflix’s cloying, candy-colored musical fantasy, Steps. Simultaneously busy and sluggish, aggressively modern and tired, this charm-deficient Cinderella makeover squanders the considerable talents of a starry voice cast on lame dialogue laced with unfunny humor in a take on the classic fairy tale so convoluted that just following the plot becomes a chore.

Produced by Amy Poehler and co-directed by Alyce Tzue and John Ripa, Steps rehashes once-radical, now familiar tropes like the antagonist as hero and the bonds of sisterhood outweighing conventional romance. In other words, ho hum. The screenplay is credited to five writers and the story to six, originating with comedy folk duo Riki Lindhome and Kate Micucci, who go by the professional name Garfunkel and Oates. That outsize assembly feeds the impression of a project patched together by committee, sucking any spark or spontaneity or emotional uplift out of the material. Related Stories Business Netflix's Bid to Buy Radford Studio Lot Stalls TV 'Nobody Wants This' Renewed for Season 4 at Netflix Steps The Bottom Line Strictly for tots through tweens. Release date: Friday, Nov. 20Cast: Amanda Seyfried, Ali Wong, Stephanie Hsu, Nikki Glaser, Daniel Radcliffe, Young Mazino, Peter Dinklage, Bette Midler, Damian Priest, Gwendoline Yeo, Matt LoweDirectors: Alyce Tzue, John RipaScreenwriters: Ava Tramer, James Madejski, Jen Chuck, Dana Schwartz, Felicia Ho Rated PG, 1 hour 41 minutes

The central conceit is that Cinderella’s stepsisters are not evil, just misunderstood. Punky teen Lilith (Ali Wong) is determined to set the record straight on what she calls the “character assassination” of the storybook tale, carving out her own “happily ever after,” while her younger sister Margot (Stephanie Hsu) is too sweet and innocent to be tainted by that traditional lore; she loves everyone, especially frogs.

When their dressmaker mother Li Mei (Gwendoline Yeo) falls in love and moves them to the kingdom of Alouette, Lilith sees it as a place where she might finally belong. Mom’s new husband Edmund (Matt Lowe) is kind and welcoming, as is his cheerful daughter Cinderella (Amanda Seyfried), with whom they form a fast friendship. But the sisters’ cheongsams and black hair instantly mark them as outsiders in their macaroon-hued new environment, at odds with the prevailing 18th century French rococo aesthetic. The abundance of pink hurt my eyes.

The bigger hurdle comes when they accompany Cinderella to a tea party hosted by vain mean girl Priscilla (Nikki Glaser), whose parents placed her in the hyper-protective care of sinister butler Roderick (Peter Dinklage). When Lilith starts entertaining the other guests with an exotic folktale, Priscilla perceives a threat to her Queen Bee domination. She tricks Lilith and Margot into entering a gated storm drain, where she locks them in. Cinderella is torn between loyalty to her mistreated stepsisters and the peer pressure of the popular kids. Naturally, she goes with the latter, which plants the festering seed of Cinderella’s betrayal.

Cinderella, by the way, is not condemned here to a life of drudgery. Instead, she obsessively cleans the house for her own pleasure, aided by a happy assortment of forest creatures. Either she’s a pathological neat freak or she’s seen Amy Adams in Enchanted a hundred times, not that it matters much.

When members of the court distribute invitations to a Royal Ball, Lilith and Margot are left off the list, to the gleeful mockery of Priscilla. They resign themselves to a quiet night at home while the rest of the town is partying. But when Lilith sees Cinderella being kitted out for the ball by her Fairy Godmother (Bette Midler), who has spent her life planning for the girl’s marriage to Alouette’s handsome Prince (Daniel Radcliffe), she hatches her own plan.

That involves snatching the Fairy Godmother’s wand and creating her own goth-glamour fantasy. Arriving in a skull-shaped black carriage that looks straight out of Tim Burton, Lilith and Margot crash the ball and liven up the party until disaster strikes.

When it starts looking like Cinderella in her glass slippers has the royal marriage sewn up, treacherous Priscilla gets hold of the wand. Priscilla uses her newfound magic to convince the Prince she’s his one true love, breaking the fairy tale. She sets the vicious Roderick — adopting the Tom Cruise sprinter style of intense concentration and rigid hands slicing the air — on the heels of Cinderella, Lilith and Margot, who gets turned into a talking frog during the confusion.

Will Cinderella be able to wind back the clock and get to the Prince by midnight, in time to prove she’s the one? Will she and Lilith patch up their differences and discover the rewards of true sisterhood? Will Margot revert to human form before losing herself as a frog? And will Lilith stumble onto her own true love when their journey through the woods takes them to the house of a hunky troll named Gef (Young Mazino), cast out by his mean brother Craktor (WWE star Damian Priest) for being too soft-hearted?

The answers are plain to see, but Tzue and Ripa spend an inordinate amount of time getting there as the forest odyssey drags on and the movie grows more stubbornly unmagical by the minute.

It’s a pleasure to hear Seyfried’s crystalline vocals on Justin Trantor’s original songs, and Midler is an old hand at cracking wise. But the distinguished voice cast mostly is given little to make their characters endearing, let alone enchanting.

The Asian elements carry through to the K-animation-meets-Disney character designs, with lots of round faces and saucer eyes, giving the impression that Netflix is trying to ride the KPop Demon Hunters wave; and to a poppy end credits song by Blackpink member Jennie. But the cross-cultural differences are too tentatively explored to gain traction, ultimately seeming kind of random and giving Steps a vibe that’s more ersatz than original. Still, children — young girls, especially — should be fine with that.

2027 Chevy Trax's $2,000 Price Increase Makes Sense

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

It's always a bummer to see a cheap car get more expensive, even if it still remains a relatively cheap vehicle, and that just happened to the Chevy Trax. The sub-compact crossover is getting a $2,000 price increase to go along with its facelift for the 2027 model year, and I'm here to tell you that it actually makes sense. You can relax. It's OK.

The Trax might cost 8.5% more than it did a year ago, now starting at $25,495 (including destination) for the base LT trim, but a big chunk of that increase comes from the fact that the previous base model, the LS, has been dropped, according to Car and Driver. Chevy has trimmed the Trax's vast lineup from five trim levels to three. In any case, along with the new entry-spec LT, the Activ and RS trims (now condensed into one trim versus the old 1RS and 2RS) will soldier on, both starting at $28,295.

I doubt anyone was really buying the old base Trax LS, anyway. People have standards. Everyone likes to talk about how much they enjoy a cheap vehicle, but at the end of the day, very few base-model vehicles actually get sold. Far more often than not, buyers will go for mid-grade trims on cheap cars, because nobody wants to be traveling around in a tin can, even if it is a good value.

The added money and higher base trim also means there's more equipment that comes as standard on the 2027 Trax. Chevy's given it a relatively handsome facelift with new headlights and all-LED taillights, which is worth a few bucks if you ask me, but there's also plenty of other features that now come as standard. Inside, there's now a standard 11-inch digital gauge cluster and 11.3-inch center infotainment screen running Google Built-in. This seems like a vast improvement over the analog gauges of the old LS, for sure. Hell, even the higher-end Trax only had an 8-inch gauge cluster before.

The little crossover also has automatic intersection emergency braking, automated reverse braking, front park assist, rear cross-traffic braking, rear seatbelt indicator, safety seat alert and side bicyclist alert. All in all, that's not a bad deal for a car that starts under $25,500, and if you agree with my sentiment, Chevy says the 2027 Trax will be available sometime in the spring of 2027. I know, it's a shame when a car gets more expensive, but usually it just happens because an automaker thinks of a bigger number. At least this time, there's a good reason behind it.

This is great news, but is there a catch?

It’s no secret that Heated Rivalry is one of the biggest book-to-screen adaptations of recent years. The smutty, queer hockey romance stole the hearts of fans everywhere when it was released in late 2025, and its popularity hasn’t wavered at all. Fans are only going to get more intense now that news has dropped that Hudson Williams and Connor Storrie will be suiting up for a third season as well. However, I'm concerned about one detail.

Fans of Heated Rivalry have already known about Season 2 kicking off production, as photos from the set have been leaked despite the show’s creator, Jacob Tierney, kindly asking fans to give them space while filming. The news about a third season has been highly speculated, and now, that has been confirmed as well. Both seasons are reportedly being filmed back-to-back. This theoretically means that there shouldn’t be a huge wait between seasons, and maybe fans could get all twelve episodes sometime next year.

In addition to two more seasons with Shane and Ilya, Deadline reports that Tierney is working on a standalone theatrical release that the trade believes will be a “Christmas special” or “holiday-themed episode” centered around Season 1, Episode 3 couple Scott (François Arnaud) and Kip (Robbie G.K.). It’s not the Heated Rivalry-centric bonus episode fans have been hoping for, but the holiday special could be inspired by the short holiday story author Rachel Reid penned for the couple and earlier this year took down from her official website.

As a fan of the sports drama series, I’m overjoyed at the news, but I do have one concern heading into these news seasons when it comes to what it might mean for Shane and Ilya’s relationship.

Unlike the other couples in Reid’s Game Changers book universe, Shane and Ilya’s relationship unfolds over several books. When the series was released (and subsequently renewed for a second and a secret third season), the couple’s second book, The Long Game, was the final installment in their relationship. But then Reid announced she was writing a third book, Unrivaled, centered on the fan-favorite couple.

Had Reid not made the announcement that she was writing Unrivaled, I would have confidently said that Tierney and the creative team behind Heated Rivalry planned to split The Long Game into two seasons. This would allow the show to really dig deep into Shane and Ilya’s relationship challenges and give fans a faithful adaptation like the first season did. It would also provide space for the show to explore some of the other couples in the Game Changes universe, which seems to be happening based on casting announcements for Season 2.

Splitting The Long Game into two seasons might still be the case, which is great news for fans of the novel; however, it does raise concerns about the potential for an adaptation of Unrivaled. After all, if Seasons 2 and 3 are centered on the second novel, then the show would need to be renewed for a fourth season in order to see the third book and the presumed conclusion of Shane and Ilya’s love story on screen.

Given the show’s popularity, a hypothetical fourth season seems like a no-brainer, but one also has to consider the schedules of the cast and crew. With Storrie and Williams both cast in new shows and movies, it might be hard to get them on board for another season beyond these next two. Still, I’d like to believe that the talented actors would be eager to see Ilya and Shane's storyline through to the end.

All of this is purely speculation, as we don't know much about Season 2 of Heated Rivalry other than it's expected to drop in Spring 2027. When that happens, we'll know what to expect from Season 3. Until then, I’ll be streaming, or as fans have dubbed it “reheating,” Season 1 of Heated Rivalry with my HBO Max subscription. Fans can also catch the episodes playing for a limited time in theaters in the US and Canada.

Not Regular Butter: Popcorn Is Crispier When You Reach For This Ingredient

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

Few snacks welcome modifications more readily than popcorn. You can go with a sweet cinnamon sugar recipe or a batch of spicy, chile-powder-covered kernels. Regardless of which direction you tweak the dish, fat is always welcome — it helps keep your seasoning on, but it's also simply delicious. Butter is the classic choice, but don't sleep on ghee, either. This foodstuff makes for an even crispier batch of the iconic movie theater snack.

Also a dairy-based fat, ghee is clarified, which means the ingredient contains less water and more fat solids than butter. Ghee is further differentiated from clarified butter by way of the toasting process, which gives ghee a pleasant nutty flavor. Together, such qualities enhance popcorn in both taste and texture. Amplified crunch is the main draw. Once melted and applied to kernels, this translates to a much less soggy consistency. The popcorn still takes on a signature buttery flavor, but with less moisture clinging to each bite, the increased fat composition turns the snack extra crispy.

Thankfully, working ghee into a batch of popcorn is straightforward. You can readily buy a jar of ghee on platforms like Amazon or Walmart, add around a tablespoon per a third cup of kernels, then pop away into a reimagined crunchy bite. It's a simple twist with a satisfying payoff.

Ghee popcorn doesn't need much to shine; a simple sprinkle of salt rounds out a mouthwatering rendition of the snack. However, if you're a fan of a more complex popcorn rendition, don't shy away from bolder seasonings, too. Ghee is often used in South Asian cuisine, which means Indian spices are a natural start. The nutty notes make a terrific canvas for warm flavors like cinnamon, coriander, and cumin. Or for an even more dessert-like spin, mix in saffron, cardamom, and fennel — spices traditionally used to flavor nuts. Whipping up such blends is like making a spice-infused oil: You'll want to slowly bloom the components in the ghee.

That said, don't rule out drawing from other cuisines with ghee, too. The milk-based fat can be the foundation of a smoky-sweet seasoning flavored with chile peppers and vanilla. Or you can incorporate a zesty complexity by using citrus rind instead. In addition to crisping up popcorn, ghee works as a remarkable flavor canvas, readily enveloping the snack in aromatics.

Onchain data casts doubt on insider-trading theory in NYT’s ‘suspicious’ election betting story

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

A New York Times story raised questions about a Polymarket trader's winning Senate bets, but The Block's analysis reveals a broader trading history that suggests other explanations for the wagers.

The New York Times wrote Friday that Vanderbilt University researchers had flagged large, profitable bets on Polymarket and Kalshi that were placed before certain polls on the Nebraska and Michigan Senate races were released. The polls favored the candidates the trader had backed, raising questions about whether the trader had advance access to the results.

However, The Block's own analysis of onchain records and trading data found that the trader has a long history of both large, risky trades and complex DeFi yield-farming that was unreported by the Times. Matching onchain deposits to Kalshi trades also suggests that the trader may have placed two large bets 10 minutes after one poll in question was published.

The trader held on to the bets all the way to Election Day rather than taking profits from short-term moves in the markets in the wake of the polls, and placed a bet on the race in Michigan while the Times/Siena polling was still underway, facts that the Times acknowledged in its report.

Those facts complicate the theory that the trader's profits resulted from advance access to unpublished Times/Siena polls. Two experts who reviewed The Block's findings said that while foul play can't be definitively ruled out, other explanations, like the trader being a crypto whale with a proclivity for large directional bets, fit the evidence better.

The Times story acknowledged it was unclear whether the trader had inside information. The researchers offered other possible explanations, and Polymarket told the paper it was conducting its own investigation.

Rajiv Sethi, an economics professor at Barnard College who reviewed The Block's findings, said the activity looked more like bets on the eventual election outcomes than an attempt to profit from the publication of the Times/Siena polls.

"I think these were just directional bets. Maybe they were based on non-public information such as privately commissioned surveys, but I don't think the Times/Siena polls were the information source," Sethi said.

The Vanderbilt researchers identified a Polymarket user who bet $191,277 that Sen. Pete Ricketts, a Nebraska Republican, would win re-election. Most of that money was wagered in the early hours of Oct. 31, roughly an hour before a Times/Siena poll published at 5:05 a.m. ET showed Ricketts ahead by six points. The user ultimately made more than $77,000 on the race, according to the Times.

The same user bet $622,608 on a Democratic victory in Michigan's Senate race. At around 7:53 p.m. ET on Nov. 1, the Times published polls of Michigan and six other states showing Michigan Democrat Abdul El-Sayed ahead by 11 points. The user made more than $180,000 on that race when El-Sayed won the seat.

The Block's analysis of Polymarket's public records matched those bets to an account named dubdubdub2. On Oct. 31, it bought $349,135 of "Yes" shares on a Democratic win in Michigan at 3:27 a.m. and $273,473 of "No" shares on a Republican win at 5:20 a.m., which are essentially bets in the same direction. Together, the two bets match the Michigan total reported by the Times.

The bets were unusually large for those markets. The account's Michigan purchases were the largest daily total by one account in any of the 16 Senate races The Block analyzed back to mid-September, and were nearly three times the next largest.

"There's a series of circumstances here that it's hard to write it off as a random occurrence," Brett Goldstein, who co-leads the prediction markets research team at Vanderbilt's Institute of National Security, told the Times. He said the activity "requires further investigation."

But while the Polymarket trades stood out on the platform, The Block's review of the trader's onchain history suggests the size of the bets was not unusual for the trader, and the specific details of some trades cut against the theory that they were exploiting advance knowledge of the polls.

Michigan also wasn't the race the Nov. 1 polls moved most. That release covered seven states, and the biggest market reaction came in Kansas, where Polymarket's odds of a Democratic win jumped from about 52.5% to 60.5% within half an hour. In Michigan, where Democrats were already the strongest favorites at about 74.5%, the odds rose to about 79.5% over two hours.

The trader did not bet on Kansas. Its market was much smaller, however, with about $1.9 million traded on a Democratic win through the election, compared with about $7.9 million in Michigan.

The researchers told the Times that the trader sent large sums to Kalshi and that large bets on similar markets followed, but the report didn't identify those bets.

The Block traced funds from wallets linked to the Polymarket account to a crypto payment provider used by Kalshi, then compared those deposits with Kalshi's public trade records. The records suggest the trader was betting on the same races on both platforms in the same directions, including possible bets placed after the Nebraska poll became public.

The account's Senate bets were funded by a separate wallet that also traded on Hyperliquid. The Block linked that wallet to the one controlling dubdubdub2 through a history of transfers between connected addresses. Its five transfers to dubdubdub2's Polymarket deposit address on Oct. 30 and 31, totaling $850,000, also match the account's deposit records to the dollar.

At 5:29 p.m. ET on Oct. 30, the betting wallet withdrew its remaining $2.2 million from Hyperliquid, where it had been trading over the previous day. About a minute later, it began sending funds to a deposit address that forwards money to a wallet labeled by blockchain analytics firm Arkham and Arbiscan as belonging to Zero Hash.

Kalshi lists Zero Hash among its crypto deposit providers. The betting wallet sent about $1.39 million to the deposit address through Oct. 31. The Block found no earlier transfers to Zero Hash from the trader's known wallets.

Kalshi's data does not link specific trades to specific accounts. However, since the transfers were consistent with Kalshi deposits and six-figure bets were rare in Kalshi's Nebraska and Michigan Senate markets, The Block was able to match certain deposits to trades by timing and size.

Several of the trader's Zero Hash deposits were followed within roughly a minute by large purchases in Kalshi's Nebraska Senate markets. On Oct. 30, a $175,000 deposit at 5:30:08 p.m. was followed 31 seconds later by about $159,000 in purchases backing Ricketts at 66 cents. A further $157,000 deposit at 5:31:58 p.m. was followed at 5:33:29 p.m. by about $169,000 in bets against Osborn at 67 cents. Both positions backed the same election outcome.

At about 5:15 a.m. on Oct. 31, 10 minutes after the Times/Siena poll was published, Kalshi recorded about $153,000 in purchases backing Ricketts to win and independent Dan Osborn to lose. The two groups of trades came eight seconds apart at prices around 75 cents, echoing the paired purchases seen earlier.

These trades are harder to attribute to the trader, however. They came about 50 minutes after a $250,000 deposit, while several earlier purchases followed deposits within roughly a minute. The deposit could also have funded bets in other markets.

If the trader did make those bets, continuing to buy in after the poll was published would suggest it was betting on the election outcome generally rather than trying to profit from the poll's short-term effect on the market, though late buys do not completely rule out the trader having advance knowledge of the poll.

Onchain transfers suggest the trader may have made more from its Kalshi activity than the roughly $258,000 its Polymarket Senate positions earned. On Nov. 4 and 5, its wallet received about $1.98 million from Zero Hash-labeled wallets, roughly $590,000 more than it had sent to Zero Hash in the first place, presumably as Kalshi deposits.

The transfers alone don't show exactly how much the trader made on Kalshi. The difference between the funds deposited and the funds received could reflect winning bets in other markets, trades that are missing from Kalshi's public records or money the trader originally deposited via a bank account.

The Times reported that the user had traded in just three Polymarket markets before October, but its story did not detail what those trades were. The Block's review found those positions were also substantial, and that holding through settlement was a recurring strategy for the trader.

The trader's dubdubdub2 account bet about $1.05 million on Zohran Mamdani winning the 2025 New York City mayoral election, about $102,000 against Andrew Cuomo in the same race and about $131,000 against the San Francisco 49ers winning the 2026 Super Bowl. It held all three positions until the markets resolved. The Block did not identify any bets that were placed shortly before mayoral polls were published.

Vincent Grégoire, a finance professor at HEC Montréal whose research includes insider trading, described the trader's history as "consistent with a wealthy individual (or small trading firm) who likes to take large bets on various platforms."

While the Times story notes that the trades were made early in the morning U.S. time, The Block's review of roughly two years of activity beginning in October 2024 found that 3 a.m. ET was the busiest hour for wallets linked to the trader. The analysis excluded Oct. 29 through Nov. 5, 2026, so the Senate bets themselves did not influence the result. It covered activity on Ethereum, Arbitrum, Base, Polygon, Hyperliquid and Polymarket, plus Solana bridge transfers.

The trader also has extensive experience within decentralized finance outside prediction markets, per The Block's analysis. The linked wallets have repeatedly farmed lending and yield protocols such as Aave and Pendle, adding up to tens of millions of dollars in total transfer volume. They have withdrawn more than $13 million in stablecoins from Kraken since October 2025. A linked Solana wallet also traded memecoins heavily in early 2025, including the official TRUMP token on its launch weekend.

The trader has also seen its share of trades gone wrong. Immediately before the election bets, the betting wallet deposited about $2.99 million in USDC on Hyperliquid and lost about $783,000 over 26 hours, including fees and funding costs, according to Hypurrscan's records for the account. The losses included about $217,000 on HYPE spot trading, $234,000 on a leveraged HYPE long and $311,000 on leveraged Intel perpetual futures positions opened after Intel reported third-quarter results.

The wallet behind dubdubdub2 also bought 60.43 million WLFI tokens for about $906,500 on the first day of the October 2024 public token sale by World Liberty Financial, the crypto venture owned by President Donald Trump's family, a purchase the Times also noted. The purchase made the wallet the largest buyer in the sale that month.

The Times said the purchase accounted for nearly one-third of all tokens sold that October. Onchain records reviewed by The Block instead put its share at about 6% of the roughly 970 million WLFI sold by the project that month, or about 9% of first-day sales. The Block has asked the Times about the discrepancy.

Grégoire said in an interview with The Block that large election bets can reflect a trader's forecasting models, private polls or other sources of information.

"To me, the simplest explanation is that there's no foul play," he said. "It's just someone that has other sources or other models."

Grégoire questioned why a polling employee would place a bet worth hundreds of thousands of dollars ahead of publication and then leave the position open rather than taking the immediate profit. He also noted that some of Polymarket's biggest historical winners made their money through a small number of large presidential election bets.

Sethi also said the use of Kalshi, which verifies traders' identities, suggests "they were not too concerned with concealing their identity."

Though The Block's analysis casts doubt on the idea that the trader had advance access to the Times/Siena polls, the reasons for the trader's bets cannot be established from the data alone.

"It's almost impossible to tell from the data; you have to know who was trading and on what information," Grégoire said, noting that it's reasonable for the institutions involved to investigate whether their processes had been compromised.

A Polymarket spokeswoman told the Times that the company spotted the trades immediately and is investigating. Kalshi declined to comment to the Times.

The Times also reported that spokespeople for the Times and the Siena Research Institute said they took the allegations seriously and planned to review the research. Times spokesperson Charlie Stadtlander said the newspaper prohibits employees from using unpublished coverage to make investments.

Lyle Durbin, CEO of ReconMR, the data collection firm that owns the Siena Research Institute, said employees are bound by confidentiality and nondisclosure agreements prohibiting the use of unpublished data for any purpose, according to the Times.

World Liberty Financial declined to comment through a representative. Kathleen Clark, a spokesperson for Vanderbilt's Institute of National Security, told The Block that Goldstein would not be able to comment. The Block also sought comment from Polymarket, Kalshi, the Times and Siena but has not heard back as of publication time.

The Times' scrutiny comes as prediction markets face broader questions about their level of oversight over their own platforms. Both Kalshi and Polymarket tightened their insider trading rules earlier this year amid scrutiny from the Senate.

In April, the Justice Department charged U.S. Army soldier Gannon Ken Van Dyke with using classified information about the operation to capture former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro to make about $409,881 on Polymarket. A former White House teleprompter operator agreed in August to pay $172,000 to settle CFTC charges that he used advance knowledge of Trump's speeches to bet on Kalshi's "mention markets."

An editor checks the facts, figures and quotes they produce against primary sources, and every piece is reviewed by an editor before it is published. Sponsored content and press releases are labeled as such and are not written by our editorial team.

Zach Cregger Revealed One Part Of His Resident Evil Experience That ‘Frustrated’ Him, And I Get It

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

I Liked Street Fighter, But I’m Not Surprised By Its Box Office Run

I Don't Think We're Going To Get A Street Fighter II, And Here's Why I'm Okay With That

I Enjoyed Backrooms, But Can We Finally Admit It Wasn't That Scary?

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

You can outrun a zombie. Good luck outrunning the internet.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Video game adaptations have a way of making everybody angry. However, I can understand why Zach Cregger expected a different conversation around his Resident Evil movie. The director behind Barbarian and Weapons has opened up about the one part of releasing his 2026 movie calendar horror hit that genuinely "frustrated" him, and I get where he's coming from.

During an appearance on The Town with Matthew Belloni, Cregger was asked whether he'd consider tackling another established franchise after Resident Evil. His answer led to a candid discussion about the reaction to his film, which follows Austin Abrams' medical courier Bryan through a nightmare in Raccoon City. Cregger felt people cared more about what he changed from Capcom's games than about the movie itself. He explained:

I think I was frustrated at the conversation around Resident Evil and how much of the conversation really didn't have anything to do with the movie. It had everything to do with whether the movie was living up to the different standards that different fans of the games had, whether it was using the characters or not, or what did I get wrong in the lore, or why is it? It was just a lot about why isn't this like the source material or why is it good that it's not like the [source material].

I can understand why that would be exhausting. After spending years making a new horror movie, you release it into the world, and suddenly everyone argues about why Leon Kennedy isn't included. However, I also empathize with fans who have waited a long time to see their favorite characters properly adapted.

Cregger chose to tell an original story within the Resident Evil universe instead of recreating one of the games, and the movie's biggest strength has nothing to do with lore or Easter eggs. His movie swaps familiar protagonists for Bryan and leans into the sensation of playing survival horror: limited resources, increasingly awful encounters and the knowledge that the next room might be your last. Before release, some fans objected to the missing characters and changes to Raccoon City.

What's interesting is that the movie did incredibly well. Resident Evil has earned more than $400 million worldwide, according to Box Office Mojo, while Rotten Tomatoes lists a 96% critics' score. Early reviews praised RE for its relentless pacing and the way Cregger captured the experience of playing the games.

Still, success didn't erase Cregger's disappointment with the discussion surrounding it. He continued:

All the conversation just felt like it was about not the movie. And I'm proud of the movie. I really love the movie. I should have known better going into it. I should have understood that that was going to be the case, but for some reason I was a little shocked.

That last admission is what stuck with me. Cregger is a fan himself, and he previously acknowledged that players hoping for Leon's story had a fair reason to feel disappointed. But he also wanted people to judge the actual movie he made. Surely there's room for both opinions.

Zach Cregger's not necessarily finished with Raccoon City, either. He told Belloni he'd like some creative input if the series continues, even if somebody else directs the next installment. Personally, I hope he gets that chance. Given how well this one turned out, I'd much rather see what else he dreams up than spend another round arguing about which character didn't show up.
